# First executions

**What this register is for.** `CLAUDE.md` carries a standing policy: *a mechanism's first execution reports
back into the record that decided it, in place, including when it found nothing.* The policy is justified by a
ratio — how many mechanisms have been executed for the first time, and how many of those held a defect no reading
had caught. This file is where that ratio is **read**, not where it is asserted.

**The invariant: one row per first execution, appended, never rewritten.** The count is the number of rows. It is
written nowhere, so it cannot go stale. A correction is a **new row citing the old one**, the same shape
`docs/spec/README.md` already fixes for requirements — "a changed requirement gets a new revision, not a new
number". A row here never restates what the deciding record says; it points at it.

**What a row is.** A *mechanism* is something the architecture record decided on paper: an ADR's flow, a store,
a protocol, a harness. Its *first execution* is the first time it ran against something real. A mechanism that
ran and found nothing still gets a row — a denominator with no numerator is the whole point of keeping a ratio.

---

## Why this file exists: three counters, three numbers, no owner

Before this register there were three places counting, and on 2026-09-11 they read:

| Where | Number | What it actually counts |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `adr/0058-the-workspace-database.md` consequence 17 | **four** | the four this record itself measured — ADR-0054's two, this record's restore, and the harness built to measure it |
| `adr/0058-the-workspace-database.md`, the run-loop section (one appended sentence) | **fifty-three** | mechanisms whose first execution held a defect, project-wide, appended to since 2026-09-03 |
| `codebase.md` | **eighty** | defects found during the build, whatever found them |
| `CLAUDE.md` | fifty-three **+ four + one** | the middle one, plus the build toolchain's and the relay's |

None of them is wrong on its own terms and no two are comparable, because no two count the same thing and none
says which it counts. Two further facts make the drift concrete rather than theoretical:

- **The sequences overlap and contradict.** ADR-0058 numbers the composer and structured questions **48–53**
  (Parts AB/AC, 2026-09-07). `codebase.md` numbers Part AL's **50–52** and Part AM's **53–55** (2026-09-09) — a
  different sequence over the same integers. Wave 1's renumbering in merge order is where the collision becomes
  visible; it is not where it starts, which is Part Y on 2026-09-07. See the reconciliation at the end.
- **`CLAUDE.md` cites a number its source does not carry.** It attributes fifty-three to "ADR-0058 consequence
  17"; consequence 17 says four. The fifty-three is in a different section of that ADR, inside a single sentence
  several thousand words long that has been appended to for two months.

This register takes the **ratio** question and nothing else. `codebase.md` keeps its defect ledger; the two now
name each other and neither restates the other's number.

---

## The register

Rows are grouped exactly as the record that reported them grouped them; where a record counted several findings
as one execution, the row says so. **A row's identity is date + mechanism + deciding record, never an integer.**
The `#` column reports what a source ledger called the finding and says which ledger, because there are two and
they diverge (see the reconciliation below); this file assigns no numbers and renumbers nobody's record.
**An unlabelled number is ADR-0058's sequence; *(codebase)* marks `codebase.md`'s.** One integer of ADR-0058's
sequence has no row here: that sentence opens its count at "six times" and never identifies the fifth, so this
file leaves the hole open rather than inventing a row to fill it.

Across fourteen captures it is quiet, with **one finding, a real one**: `table-layout: fixed` fitted the worktrees table to its card and left seven columns sharing 342 px at the minimum size, six of the seven holding content wider than their 48.56 px column and painting across their neighbours. Two candidate repairs were tried and **measured afterwards**; neither moved the number, and neither was kept. The cause is the view's split, which is the design material's call. Two lessons the check itself taught: an **ancestor's** scroll is no excuse (`.git` is `overflow: auto`, and scrolling never revealed the header's second line because the next card paints over it), and an opt-in contract catches nothing where nobody thought to publish it — `text-fit` existed all along and was on no header |

The generator a test session runs replaced the artifact with a single `writeFileSync` **over the target** — no staging, no rename, no ladder — so a concurrent reader could observe a partial artifact, which `REQ-TRACE-502` forbids in terms. And the adapter did not catch: a throw out of vitest's `globalSetup` fails the whole run before one test is collected, which `REQ-TRACE-182` says shall never happen |
| **2026-09-13** | **the plan gate, built** — the planning phase as a run segment, its three enforcement points and its durable half (`pnpm plan-gate-proof`, schema v17) | **`adr/0062-the-plan-gate-as-a-run-segment.md`** / `REQ-ORCH-152` to `REQ-ORCH-154` | — *(unnumbered; this work package's own findings)* | **the event specification contradicted the requirement it cites.** A4's round widened `REQ-HOOKS-126`'s skip-reason set to two members; `REQ-EVENTS-047` and the `hook.finish` row of the payload table in `20-event-history-replay-export.md` both still say `untrusted_workspace` is *the only defined value*, so a reader validating against the event document alone would reject a conformant `plan_gate` event. Recorded as a defect found by building, and it owes an amendment round. **The built-app proof (`pnpm plan-gate-proof`) found a second defect, and it was in the surface this Part drew**: every behavioural assertion held first time — the pending decision and the user's edit came back from the store with the process that wrote them gone, and no file the plan named existed at any held launch — but **five consecutive captures each left some part of the gate unreachable**, and the fifth was the first where a scripted click on *Approve* actually landed. Each attempt named the same mistake in different clothes and each repair moved the casualty rather than removing it: sticky inside a tail-following log scrolled the title away, a `vh` bound measured the window instead of the column, `max-height: 100%` and then `min-height: 0` left a flex child that would not shrink, its own grid row was allocated less than its content and the transcript painted over the answers, and lowering the transcript's floor still left the buttons twenty pixels under the divider. The repair that holds is not arithmetic: the three answers are `position: sticky; bottom: 0` inside the block, so a decision the run is stopped on cannot depend on a layout sum coming out right at one window size. This is UIR-014's class — a box standing smaller than the content inside it — and **no unit test in 1,639 could have seen it**; only the pixels could. The harness taught one thing too: a synthetic `blur` reaches no React handler, because React delegates `onBlur` from `focusout`. Two things the record expected to find were **not** defects: `withholdsOn` already delivered the fail-closed reading for every MCP and plugin name with no new machinery, and the root execution record's missing identity (a defect note, removed once repaired) turned out not to block the gate at all — the note's claim that it is *the first thing that Part will need* is wrong, because nothing in a plan cycle looks the root up by name |

The audit now records the effective permission and captured runtime. Overlapping launches retain their own grants and credentials; a refused active-run prompt returns before configuration loading. |

The unit suite could not see it because the only path a model reaches had no test. Found while planning the directive's closeout, repaired by filtering against contracts' `ARTIFACT_SECTIONS` (the single definition), pinned by a byte-exact regression through `artifactOps.read`, and held shut by the built-app launch: the ledger lands in the exact order `node --version` (0), `node -e "process.exit(7)"` (**failed, exit 7**), `rm -rf build` (exit status not recorded — the gate's denial carries no `[terminal_diagnostic]` block, which is how REQ-TOOLS-100's dropped null key reaches the ledger), checked in SQLite, through the real filtered tool read, in the sidecar through the stock parser, and in the rendered inspector |

The UI-language guard's CSS scans ran over raw text, so a property name in prose inside a `/* … */` matched past the comment's end and attributed the next rule's literal to the sentence; stripping comments before the scans left the committed baseline agreeing with the corrected counts exactly, which the ratchet's over-count-only direction predicted. Every clause is true and the conclusion is not: **dash forks the command too** — 5,306 ms for the same 60 s sleep under the same 0.3 s budget, against cmd.exe's 5,334 ms, with the same empty output. The interpreter is always the started process and the command is always its child *on every platform*; whether killing the one reaches the other is the SHELL's choice, since a `/bin/sh` that execs a simple command dies with it and resolves at once keeping its output. So a timed-out hook's cost, and whether partial output survives, vary by which shell `/bin/sh` is — inside `REQ-HOOKS-033`'s bound and permitted by it in terms — and the proof now asserts the bound rather than the cost. The skill search's `/etc/codex/skills` has no seam, so `skills-proof`, `injection-proof` and the catalog tests still read the machine's own, and `settings-proof` does not yet set the instruction seam: recorded, not repaired ([`three-proofs-and-the-catalog-tests-read-the-machines-own-etc-codex.md`](../defects/fixed/three-proofs-and-the-catalog-tests-read-the-machines-own-etc-codex.md)). The same verification traced a launch reading the operator's `~/.agents/prompts`; the proof now moves `HOME` into its scratch profile on POSIX. It also found REQ-WORKSPACE-045's decoding half unmet (a file that is not UTF-8 is merged with no warning), which predates this work: recorded, not repaired ([`an-instruction-file-that-is-not-utf-8-is-injected-with-no-warning.md`](../defects/fixed/an-instruction-file-that-is-not-utf-8-is-injected-with-no-warning.md)). **What the proof found in the product: nothing wrong.** Order, presence and absence (not even a source comment) all held. So did the on override's suppression of its own base, OQ-64 as ruled, the context ahead of the skill catalog, and an isolated run reading the committed text under its worktree's paths. Two falsifications were built and run, and each turned the proof red: suppression computed over presence, and context read from the base root. **The reading (Linux, 4 vCPU Xeon @ 2.10 GHz, Firecracker VM, virtio disk).** Every fixture page was confirmed non-resident by `mincore(2)` before each run; cold means the guest's page cache, not the hypervisor's. **p95 20.29 ms** over 20 runs, retaken after the gate's fixes with its evidence on disk; median 11.16 ms, maximum 21.34 ms; **met**. Three earlier runs of 20 gave p95 15.42 ms (first reported here; its evidence was since cleared), 25.4 ms and 28.64 ms, so the verdict does not rest on the best run. The window's responsiveness while the merge runs is argued from placement (the run host), not measured. All 20 processes produced byte-identical merged context, and each merge ran only after the prompt was recorded as row 0. **The Windows gate-class reading is owed**, with a Windows cold-cache method: the script labels a non-Linux reading warm and does not assess it. |

all five cases red before the repair. **Found the note's reading question settled by the specification**: REQ-HOOKS-075 lists `blocked` and `cancelled` among the hook's states. **Found a first test wrong about which path it reached**: a running child cancelled by the explicit cancel is ended by its own execution, which already fired the hook, so the finalize case was rewritten around a child still waiting for a model slot. **Found that case could not pin the run's wait for the sweep's hooks** — the explicit cancel's `outlast` awaits them as executions — so a sixth case holds the hook of a child the sweep cancels at an ordinary exit (the root's turn failed); Not executed in the built app: no proof or live-run scenario configures a lifecycle hook. **Found by the repair's verification, and closed with it**: a settle's end removed its child's `settles` entry whatever settle had put it there, so a child followed up while its cancel's hook still ran fired `child_completed` a third time when that hook ended mid-way through the follow-up's own; `beginSettle`'s end now removes only its own entry ~~(a seventh case, red before)~~ (that seventh case was red before, but since the second verification's `tellingOwnSettle` it no longer pins the guard, *corrected by the third verification*: the guard now keeps the later end settling to a parent's `settlesOf`, pinned by a tenth case, red without it). ~~The stop's grace bounds these hooks as it bounds a settle's own, stated on the note.~~ (*Corrected by the fourth verification*: the note's "a pause waits for it" was false — see below.) **Found by the second verification, and closed with it**: a hook queued behind one still running read the child's record when it started, so a cancelled end whose child was followed up and succeeded meanwhile was sent as `succeeded`, twice (falsified: "the fields are the child's record once it is terminal"); the fields are now taken as the scheduler is told. The listener's guard, a `settles` entry, also hid a second outside end while the first's hook was queued; it is now `tellingOwnSettle`. Two cases, each red before. **Found by the third verification, and closed with it**: nothing pinned that a parent the cancel, the cascade or the sweep wakes waits for the outside hook before its next model step — every case stayed green with `completedOutside` holding no `settles` entry; an eleventh case holds a cancelled child's hook and is red without it. The terminal listener's read of the record now falls back to the moved row when the store raises, as `afterTerminal`'s does (a twelfth case, which fired nothing before). **Found by the fourth verification, and closed with it**: a rest waited on a snapshot of the executions taken when it began, and the run's rest branch never waited for the outside hooks, so a child the user cancelled while a pause or a stop waited, or a paused child cancelled once the rest was recorded, had its `child_completed` end after `session_end` (falsified: "a pause waits for it"; recorded order `session_start`, `child_completed(b)`, `session_end`, then the hook's end). The rest's wait now looks again until no execution is left, and the run waits for the outside hooks before `session_end`, within the stop's remaining grace after a stop; three cases, each red before and each red with only its own part removed. Found beside it and recorded, not closed: a cancel of one child accepted while `session_end`'s hooks run fires its hook after `session_end` ([`a-child-cancelled-while-session-end-runs-fires-child-completed-after-it`](../defects/fixed/a-child-cancelled-while-session-end-runs-fires-child-completed-after-it.md)). |

Item 4b's "logged after the confirm click" went red from its own sampling race (a probe logged in the click's millisecond read `Running` before the renderer handled the click); each sample is now one snapshot gated on the confirmation's close. A poll of a queued call handed the earlier command's output over as its own. The interrupt now marks every open paused call, and a queued call a reset closes before it ran is reported as never run, naming the reset; two real-PTY cases, red before, pin both. **Could not show:** Windows, where nothing here depends on the shell but no host has run it. |
